62em){.__m__-_R_2muqssm_{--col-flex-grow:auto;--col-flex-basis:58.333333333333336%;--col-max-width:58.333333333333336%;}}</style><div class="m_96bdd299 mantine-Grid-col __m__-_R_2muqssm_"><article class="blog-article-body"><address class="article-author visually-hidden">By <span><a rel="author" href="/blog/authors/threat-research-team">Threat Research Team</a></span></address><div style="--container-size:var(--container-size-xxl);max-width:calc(97.5rem * var(--mantine-scale));padding-inline:0rem" class="m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-size="xxl" data-strategy="block" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>We recently came across a stealer, called <code>Raccoon Stealer,</code> a name given to it by its author. Raccoon Stealer uses the <code>Telegram</code> infrastructure to store and update actual <code>C&amp;C</code> addresses.&nbsp;</p><p>Raccoon Stealer is a password stealer capable of stealing not just passwords, but various types of data, including:</p><ul><li>Cookies, saved logins and forms data from browsers</li><li>Login credentials from email clients and messengers</li><li>Files from crypto wallets</li><li>Data from browser plugins and extension</li><li>Arbitrary files based on commands from C&amp;C</li></ul><p>In addition, it’s able to download and execute arbitrary files by command from its C&amp;C. In combination with active development and promotion on underground forums, Raccoon Stealer is prevalent and dangerous.</p><p>The oldest samples of Raccoon Stealer we’ve seen have timestamps from the <code>end of April 2019</code>. Its authors have stated the same month as the start of selling the malware on underground forums. Since then, it has been updated many times. According to its authors, they fixed bugs, added features, and more.</p><h2><strong>Distribution</strong></h2><p>We’ve seen Raccoon distributed via downloaders: <code>Buer Loader</code> and <code>GCleaner</code>. According to some samples, we believe it is also being distributed in the form of <code>fake game cheats</code>, <code>patches for cracked software</code> (including hacks and mods for <code>Fortnite</code>, <code>Valorant</code>, and <code>NBA2K22</code>), or other software. Taking into account that Raccoon Stealer is for sale, it’s distribution techniques are limited only by the imagination of the end buyers. Some samples are spread unpacked, while some are protected using <code>Themida</code> or malware packers. Worth noting is that some samples were packed more than <a href="https://twitter.com/AvastThreatLabs/status/1420372556142432257">five times in a row</a> with the same packer!&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Technical details</strong></p><p>Raccoon Stealer is written in <code>C/C++</code> and built using <code>Visual Studio</code>. Samples have a size of about <code>580-600 kB</code>. The code quality is below average, some strings are encrypted, some are not.</p><p>Once executed, Racoon Stealer starts checking for the default user locale set on the infected device and won’t work if it’s one of the following:</p><ul><li>Russian</li><li>Ukrainian</li><li>Belarusian</li><li>Kazakh</li><li>Kyrgyz</li><li>Armenian</li><li>Tajik</li><li>Uzbek</li></ul><h2><strong>C&amp;C communications</strong></h2><p>The most interesting thing about this stealer is its communication with C&amp;Cs. There are four values crucial for its C&amp;C communication, which are hardcoded in every Raccoon Stealer sample:</p><ul><li><code>MAIN_KEY</code>. This value has been changed four times during the year.</li><li><code>URLs</code> of Telegram gates with channel name. Gates are used not to implement a complicated Telegram protocol and not to store any credentials inside samples</li><li><code>BotID</code> – hexadecimal string, sent to the C&amp;C every time</li><li><code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> – a key to decrypt the C&amp;C address obtained from Telegram Gate</li></ul><p>Let’s look at an example to see how it works:<br><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code> unpacked to:<br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code>:</p><ol><li>First of all, <code>MAIN_KEY</code> is decrypted. See the decryption code in the image below:</li></ol></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Here you can see a Telegram channel name and its status in Base64: <code>e74b2mD/ry6GYdwNuXl10SYoVBR7/tFgp2f-v32</code><br>The prefix (always five characters) and postfix (always six characters) are removed and it becomes <code>mD/ry6GYdwNuXl10SYoVBR7/tFgp</code> The Base64 is then decoded to obtain an encrypted C&amp;C URL:</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The <code>TELEGRAM_KEY</code> in this sample is a string <code>739b4887457d3ffa7b811ce0d03315ce</code> and the Raccoon uses it as a key to <code>RC4</code> algorithm to finally decrypt the C&amp;C URL: <code>http://91.219.236[.]18/</code></p><ol start="4"><li>Raccoon makes a query string with PC information (machine GUID and user name), and <code>BotID</code></li><li>Query string is encrypted with <code>RC4</code> using a <code>MAIN_KEY</code> and then encoded with Base64.</li><li>This data is sent using POST to the C&amp;C, and the response is encoded with Base64 and encrypted with the <code>MAIN_KEY</code>. Actually, it’s a JSON with a lot of parameters and it looks like this:</li></ol></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Thus, the Telegram infrastructure is used to store and update actual C&amp;C addresses. It looks quite convenient and reliable until Telegram decides to take action.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Analysis</strong></p><h2><strong>The people behind Raccoon Stealer</strong></h2><p>Based on our analysis of seller messages on underground forums, we can deduce some information about the people behind the malware. Raccoon Stealer was developed by a team, some (or maybe all) members of the team are Russian native speakers. Messages on the forum are written in Russian, and we assume they are from former USSR countries because they try to prevent the Stealer from targeting users in these countries.</p><p>Possible names/nicknames of group members may be supposed based on the analysis of artifacts, found in samples:</p><ul><li><code>C:\Users\a13xuiop1337\</code></li><li><code>C:\Users\David\&nbsp;</code></li></ul><h2><strong>Prevalence</strong></h2><p>Raccoon Stealer is quite prevalent: from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code> our systems detected <code>more than 25,000</code> Raccoon-related samples. We identified <code>more than 1,300</code> distinct configs during that period.<br>&nbsp;</p><p>Here is a map, showing the number of systems Avast protected from Raccoon Stealer from <code>March 3, 2021 - February 17, 2022</code>. In this time frame, Avast protected nearly <code>600,000</code> Raccoon Stealer attacks.</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace mantine-Container-root" data-strategy="block"><picture id="the-picture"><source 1x, 2x" src="/blog/_next/image?url=https%3A%2F%2Fwww.gendigital.com%2Fblog%2Fsites%2Fdefault%2Ffiles%2Fstyles%2Fblogs_paragraph_image_small%2Fpublic%2F2024-06%2Frs06.png.webp%3Fitok%3DL7c9xtm9&amp;w=640&amp;q=75"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The country where we have blocked the most attempts is Russia, which is interesting because the actors behind the malware don’t want to infect computers in Russia or Central Asia. We believe the attacks spray and pray, distributing the malware around the world. It’s not until it makes it onto a system that it begins checking for the default locale. If it is one of the language listed above, it won’t run. This explains why we detected so many attack attempts in Russia, we block the malware before it can run, ie. before it can even get to the stage where it checks for the device’s locale. If an unprotected device that comes across the malware with its locale set to English or any other language that is not on the exception list but is in Russia, it would stiIl become infected.&nbsp;</p></div><div style="padding-inline:0rem;margin-block:var(--mantine-spacing-md)" class="imageWithCaption_container__fP6ro imageWithCaption_fullWidth__4Ncwy m_7485cace class="mantine-focus-auto bodyText imageWithCaption_caption__hCQ67 picture-caption m_b6d8b162 mantine-Text-root">Screenshot with claims about not working with CIS</p></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Telegram Channels</strong></h2><p>From the more than 1,300 distinct configs we extracted, 429 of them are unique Telegram channels. Some of them were used only in a single config, others were used dozens of times. The most used channels were:</p><ul><li><code>jdiamond13</code> – 122 times</li><li><code>jjbadb0y</code> – 44 times</li><li><code>nixsmasterbaks2</code> – 31 times</li><li><code>hellobyegain</code> – 25 times</li><li><code>h_smurf1kman_1&nbsp;</code> – 24 times</li></ul><p>Thus, five of the most used channels were found in about <code>19%</code> of configs.</p><h2><strong>Malware distributed by Raccoon</strong></h2><p>As was previously mentioned, Raccoon Stealer is able to download and execute arbitrary files from a command from C&amp;C. We managed to collect some of these files. We collected <code>185 files</code>, with a total size <code>265 Mb</code>, and some of the groups are:</p><ul><li><code>Downloaders</code> – used to download and execute other files</li><li><code>Clipboard crypto stealers</code> – change crypto wallet addresses in the clipboard – very popular (more than 10%)</li><li><code>WhiteBlackCrypt Ransomware</code></li></ul><h3><strong>Servers used to download this software</strong></h3><p>We extracted unique links to other malware from Raccoon configs received from C&amp;Cs, it was <code>196 unique URLs</code>. Some analysis results:</p><ul><li><code>43% </code>of URLs have <code>HTTP</code> scheme, <code>57%</code> – <code>HTTPS</code>.</li><li><code>83 domain names</code> were used.</li><li>About <code>20% of malware</code> were placed on <code>Discord CDN</code></li><li>About <code>10%</code> were served from <code>aun3xk17k[.]space</code></li></ul><p><strong>Conclusion</strong></p><p>We will continue to monitor Raccoon Stealer’s activity, keeping an eye on new C&amp;Cs, Telegram channels, and downloaded samples. We predict it may be used wider by other cybercrime groups. We assume the group behind Raccoon Stealer will further develop new features, including new software to steal data from, for example, as well as bypass protection this software has in place.</p><h2><strong>IoC</strong></h2><p><code>447c03cc63a420c07875132d35ef027adec98e7bd446cf4f7c9d45b6af40ea2b</code><br><code>f1cfcce14739887cc7c082d44316e955841e4559ba62415e1d2c9ed57d0c6232</code></p></div><p And while there have been many other changes in Vidar lately, with new versions dropping every week, in this blog post we focus solely on the ABE bypass and its technical aspects.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe ABE Bypass\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFrom a high-level perspective, Vidar’s approach to bypassing ABE is somewhat similar to that of Remus/Lumma, in that both try to extract the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e directly from the browser’s memory. What sets them apart, however, is how they achieve it. Since we’ve already covered the inner workings of ABE in earlier posts (\u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/voidstealer-abe-bypass\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eVoidStealer\u003c/a\u003e), we don’t revisit them here and focus instead on the bypass itself. For readers interested in the broader background, we encourage checking those earlier posts.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn short, the end goal of infostealers is to obtain the \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e, as it alone is sufficient for decrypting any ABE-protected data tied to a specific application. This key can be obtained in one of two ways: either from disk, where it is protected by two layers of \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectData\u003c/code\u003e (one applied in the context of the logged-in user and one as \u003ccode\u003eNT AUTHORITY\\SYSTEM\u003c/code\u003e), or from the browser’s memory – the direction Vidar took.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn memory, however, the key is kept protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using the \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e flag. This ensures that even if an attacker manages to read the encrypted form of the key from memory, they cannot simply decrypt it (they would need to invoke the complementary \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e function directly from within the browser process). That leaves attackers with two problems to solve: how to find the encrypted key in memory, and how to get it decrypted.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eLocating the v20_master_key in Browser’s Memory\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eVidar’s approach to locating the encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e in memory involves scanning the entire memory of a running browser for a specific pattern. But that first requires a browser process to scan.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen the target browser is already running, Vidar reuses the existing process. However, it doesn’t read its memory directly. Instead, it creates a fork of it using \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003eSectionHandle\u003c/code\u003e set to \u003ccode\u003eNULL\u003c/code\u003e, instructing the kernel to clone the address space of the process specified by the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e as a copy-on-write mapping. The resulting process has no threads and is never resumed – it exists purely as a static memory snapshot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTo perform the fork, Vidar first opens a handle to the target browser via \u003ccode\u003eOpenProcess\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_QUERY_INFORMATION | PROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS | PROCESS_VM_READ\u003c/code\u003e, falling back to \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_CREATE_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e if the initial call fails. It then passes this handle as the \u003ccode\u003eParentProcess\u003c/code\u003e argument to \u003ccode\u003eNtCreateProcessEx\u003c/code\u003e, requesting \u003ccode\u003ePROCESS_ALL_ACCESS\u003c/code\u003e on the resulting child. This voting is performed independently for each browser process, producing one best candidate per process.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDecrypting the v20_master_key via APC Injections\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor each browser process that produced a match, Vidar now holds a candidate address pointing to a potential encrypted \u003ccode\u003ev20_master_key\u003c/code\u003e. However, as already noted, since the key is protected with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e using \u003ccode\u003eCRYPTPROTECTMEMORY_SAME_PROCESS\u003c/code\u003e, it can only be decrypted by invoking \u003ccode\u003eCryptUnprotectMemory\u003c/code\u003e from within the browser process itself. Vidar solves this by injecting an Asynchronous Procedure Call (APC) into the live browser process.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eVidar supports two APC injection methods, selected based on whether ESET or Bitdefender is detected on the system. If either of these products is present, Vidar uses the first method, which it refers to as “classic” (based on decrypted strings) – it creates a suspended thread in the target browser via \u003ccode\u003eCreateRemoteThread\u003c/code\u003e (with \u003ccode\u003eNtTestAlert\u003c/code\u003e as the thread start address and the \u003ccode\u003eCREATE_SUSPENDED\u003c/code\u003e flag), queues an APC using \u003ccode\u003eNtQueueApcThread\u003c/code\u003e, resumes the thread, and then waits for it to complete via \u003ccode\u003eWaitForSingleObject\u003c/code\u003e with a randomized timeout. When the thread resumes, it calls \u003ccode\u003eNtTestAlert\u003c/code\u003e, which flushes the APC queue and executes the injected routine.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=6gMQUMWE 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=rU7QOnfu 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":368},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=FzPY-LMw 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=aACgaVQw 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":280},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=vL6oWpc7 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_figure_08.jpg.webp?itok=0wePEmGQ 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":217}],"caption":"Figure 8: Vidar’s APC method dispatcher. To read it, Vidar creates a second fork of the browser process and reads the key from the same address using \u003ccode\u003eNtReadVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e. It then compares the result against a snapshot of the encrypted key taken from the first fork before the APC call – if the data is unchanged, decryption failed, and Vidar moves on to the next browser process’s candidate.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf the data differs, Vidar uses the same second fork to perform an additional verification: it scans the forked process’s memory for the byte sequence \u003ccode\u003ev20\u003c/code\u003e and, treating each match as a potentially ABE-encrypted entry (cookie, password), attempts AES-256-GCM decryption with the key candidate using \u003ccode\u003eBCryptDecrypt\u003c/code\u003e (successful GCM tag verification confirms that the key is correct).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhenever the data has changed (indicating that decryption of the key took place), Vidar re-encrypts the key in the browser’s memory by injecting another APC with \u003ccode\u003eCryptProtectMemory\u003c/code\u003e as the routine, preserving the browser’s state. If no candidate produced a successful decryption (i.e., the data remained unchanged after every APC call), Vidar takes a more aggressive approach: it terminates all browser instances, restarts the browser, and repeats the entire scan-decrypt-verify cycle.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBy abusing APC injections instead of the more traditional approach of injecting code via \u003ccode\u003eNtWriteVirtualMemory\u003c/code\u003e that performs the decryption, Vidar trades one suspicious behavior for another. APC injections are arguably less common and may thus slip past some detection systems, though they are still well-known techniques monitored by AV and EDR solutions. But that is the nature of ABE bypasses – each has its own advantages and disadvantages, and that’s exactly why infostealers keep inventing new ones. \u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eIndicator of Compromise (IoC)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003e459daa809751e73f60fbbe4384a7d1653c36bb06945e4eb3635270924241100a (Vidar 2.1)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"13","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/vidar_blogpost_header.jpg?h=5e1f4f74\u0026itok=ejS-nCne","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/flight-cancelled-refund-message-scam","title":"Your flight was cancelled. Is the refund message real? ","created":"1781722434","subtitle":"Travel disruption gives scammers the one thing they need most: a believable reason to rush you","authors":[{"id":"15036","socialLinks":[],"bio":"At Gen, Luis tracks evolving threats and trends, turning research into actionable safety advice. 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_author_bio_small_2x/public/blog-author-pictures/LuisCorronsHeadshot.jpg.webp?h=0c1d77dd\u0026itok=vXd2nscc 2x","media":"(min-width: have a summer flight booked. You have seen the headlines about jet fuel shortages, cancelled routes and higher travel costs. Then a text message arrives: your flight has been affected, and you need to click a link to confirm your refund or rebook before the offer expires.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat message feels plausible because the situation around it is real. In April, \u003ca href=\"https://www.iata.org/en/pressroom/2026-releases/2026-04-17-01/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eIATA warned\u003c/a\u003e that Europe could start seeing flight cancellations by the end of May if jet fuel shortages materialized. Airlines and governments have since been trying to reassure travelers, while also publishing guidance on passenger rights, refunds and re-routing.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is exactly the kind of confusion scammers like. They do not need to cause the disruption, they only need to arrive at the right moment, with the right brand logo and the right sense of urgency.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy travel disruption works so well as a scam hook\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost scam messages fail because they arrive out of context. A random refund email from an airline you never use is easy to ignore. A refund message from the airline you are flying with next week, during a period of public travel disruption, is different.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe scammer’s job is to make you think: “This could be real.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat can happen in several ways. You might receive a fake cancellation email that links to a refund form. You might search for an airline’s customer service number and land on a fake support page. You might post publicly on social media asking an airline for help, then receive a reply from an account that looks official. Or you might get a WhatsApp message that knows your hotel name, travel dates or booking reference.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eEach version uses the same pressure point: you are worried about losing money, losing your flight or being stranded.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCriminals are already using disruption this way. In March, \u003ca href=\"https://www.theguardian.com/money/2026/mar/15/travel-scam-airline-accounts-fake-refunds-iran-war-flight-disruption\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eThe Guardian reported\u003c/a\u003e that fake airline support accounts were targeting travelers affected by Middle East flight disruption. Some victims were looking for help or refunds, then received replies from accounts pretending to represent airlines. The scam moved into direct messages, where victims were asked for details and sent links that led to money being taken instead of refunded.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe jet fuel story gives scammers another pretext. A message can claim that your flight was cancelled, your fare changed, your fuel surcharge failed, or your refund must be claimed within a few hours. None of those claims needs to be true to be effective.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe scam may start before the message arrives\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake travel message becomes much harder to spot when it includes real information.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is the idea behind \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/reservation-hijack-scam\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eReservation Hijack scams\u003c/a\u003e, a form of targeted phishing where cybercriminals use real booking details to make a message look legitimate. In some cases, attackers may use information exposed through breaches or compromised travel systems. In other cases, they collect details from emails, social media posts, previous leaks or fake customer service interactions. Gen has previously described Reservation Hijack scams as scams that use real hotel booking details to make fraudulent payment or verification requests feel authentic.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat distinction matters. A scam message does not need your credit card number to fool you. Knowing your name, your destination, your travel dates and your booking platform may be enough.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat these scams can look like\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eA travel disruption scam usually does not begin with a strange offer. It begins with something boring and administrative. That is part of the trick.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eYou may see messages like:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your flight has been cancelled due to fuel supply issues. Claim your refund here.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your rebooking is pending. Confirm passenger details within two hours.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Additional airport tax or fuel surcharge is required to keep your booking.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Your hotel reservation will be cancelled unless payment is verified.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e“Due to high call volume, please continue this request on WhatsApp.”\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe exact wording changes, but the structure is familiar. There is a problem, a deadline and a link. The message may also move you away from the official channel into SMS, WhatsApp, Telegram, direct messages or a phone call. Once that happens, the scammer controls the conversation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is another detail worth watching: some messages do not ask for money immediately. They start by asking for your booking reference, last name, phone number or one-time password. That can still be dangerous. Those details can help scammers access a booking, impersonate you to customer support, reset an account or make the next message more convincing.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eYour real passenger rights can be used against you\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf an airline cancels your flight, you may have rights to a refund, re-routing or assistance, depending on where you are travelling and the rules that apply. The European Commission said passengers affected by cancellations continue to have rights to reimbursement, re-routing or return, airport assistance and compensation for last-minute cancellations in relevant circumstances. It also said airlines cannot retroactively add fuel surcharges to already published final ticket prices.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat information is useful for travelers, but it is also useful for scammers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA fake refund message becomes more believable when people already know refunds are possible. A fake rebooking link becomes more believable when airlines are genuinely changing schedules. A fake “fuel surcharge” demand becomes more dangerous when fuel prices are in the news, even though legitimate retroactive charges may not be allowed in many cases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis is why the safest route is not to judge the message only by whether the story sounds plausible. During travel disruption, many scam stories will sound plausible. Judge the channel instead.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eHow to check before you click\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the airline or booking platform’s official app or website. Do not use the link in the message. Open the app yourself, type the website address yourself, or use the contact details listed on your original booking confirmation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCheck whether the same cancellation, refund or payment request appears inside your account. If the message is real, the airline or hotel should be able to confirm it through an official channel.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe careful with social media support. If an account replies to your public complaint, check whether it is linked from the airline’s official website. Look at the handle carefully. Fake support accounts often copy logos and names, but have small differences in spelling, low follower counts or recently created profiles. The Guardian reported that fraudsters often scan social media for passengers trying to contact legitimate airline accounts and reply to them first, which makes the scam harder to spot.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDo not share one-time passwords, payment card details or login credentials with anyone who contacts you unexpectedly. A real airline or hotel does not need your banking OTP to send you a refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBe especially suspicious of pressure. “You have 30 minutes to claim your refund” is not customer service. It is a tactic.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat to do if you already clicked\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you entered payment details, contact your bank or card provider immediately. Ask whether the transaction can be blocked or reversed, and request a new card if needed.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared login details for an airline, hotel or booking platform, change the password from the official site or app. Use a unique password, not one you use elsewhere. Turn on two-factor authentication where it is available.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf you shared a one-time password or authentication code, assume the account may have been accessed. Check your booking details, payment methods and recent account activity.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReport the scam to the airline, hotel or booking platform being impersonated. If the scam came through social media, report the account as impersonation. If money was taken, report it to the relevant fraud authority in your country.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eThe real lesson for summer travel\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eTravel disruption changes how people behave. They search for support numbers in a hurry. They post publicly because customer service queues are long. They accept strange instructions because they are worried about losing a flight or refund.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers build around that behavior.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe safest assumption is simple: if a message about your flight, hotel or refund creates urgency and gives you a link, pause. Go back to the official source. A real cancellation will still be there when you check through the airline or booking platform. A scammer needs you to act before you do.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"}],"itemState":"published","timeToRead":"8","translations":[],"originalLanguagePost":{},"mediaType":{"video":false,"audio":false},"socialMediaMetaImage":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/social_media_meta/public/2026-06/threat%20flight%20canceled%20image.jpg?h=8c9c35ae\u0026itok=33YI5TgM","relatedPosts":[{"urlAlias":"/blog-post/inside-vidar-abe-bypass","title":"Inside fake job interview page is easier to trust than a fake ticket shop.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn samples reviewed by Gen threat researchers, scammers used branded hiring pages that looked like ordinary recruitment flows: a company logo, a recruiter profile, a 30-minute meeting, and a button to continue with Google or Facebook. There was no attachment to open and no software to install. The credential theft attempt sat inside a step many job applicants already expect: signing in to schedule a call.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFIFA is an obvious lure ahead of the 2026 World Cup. On May 22, ESET reported fake FIFA-themed websites that copied ticket and merchandise flows to steal money and personal data. Our researchers later found another FIFA-themed domain, hxxps://fifa-mg[.]shop, which followed a naming pattern not mentioned in that report. Domains like these can be short-lived, often appearing for hours or days before they are blocked, abandoned, or taken down.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOur researchers also found a different kind of FIFA lure: hxxps://fifahiring[.]com, a fake hiring page that encouraged users to schedule a call with an alleged FIFA recruiter. When a user tried to register with a personal Gmail address, the page asked for a business email instead. The page was steering victims toward credentials with more value: accounts that could provide access to a company used FIFA branding, presented a recruiter identity, and copied parts of the experience users see on legitimate recruiting and scheduling platforms. The recruiter’s name and photo appeared to match a real LinkedIn profile, which made the page feel more credible. For publication, we should keep that person’s identity redacted.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe domain did not match FIFA’s real hiring infrastructure. FIFA’s legitimate careers pages are jobs.fifa.com and fifa.pinpointhq.com. The fake page used neither. The domain and certificate were newly created, from May 23. The site used commodity hosting infrastructure, including Vercel and Render, rather than a corporate environment. Those signs are not enough on their own, but they fit the rest of the evidence.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe login flow was the clearest part. It was rendered inside the page itself. The visible controls and links did nothing, and the page did not send the user into a genuine Google sign-in flow. flexibility makes the campaign harder to frame as a single World Cup scam. FIFA provides the timely hook, but the mechanism is recruitment phishing. A scammer can swap the logo, recruiter name, and sign-in provider while keeping the same basic path to the victim’s credentials.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhy fake recruitment pages work\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eRecruitment gives attackers a clean excuse to ask for things that might otherwise raise suspicion.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA candidate expects to click a scheduling link. They expect a recruiter profile. They expect a calendar prompt. In some cases, they may also expect to use a work email, especially for corporate, contractor, or partner-facing roles.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake login window turns that ordinary flow into credential theft. Users are used to “Continue with Google” and “Continue with Facebook” buttons, but the button itself proves nothing. A genuine authentication flow should take the user to the provider’s domain and controls. A fake page can draw a convincing copy while keeping the victim inside the attacker-controlled site.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe business email prompt is also a warning sign. A page that rejects a personal email and asks for a work address may be trying to raise the value of the stolen account.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat job seekers should check before signing in\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eStart from the company’s official careers page, not from a link in a message, ad, search result, or forwarded post. For FIFA, that means checking jobs.fifa.com or fifa.pinpointhq.com, not trusting a domain because it contains “fifa,” “career,” “hiring,” or “jobs.”\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eLook closely at the domain before entering credentials. Scam pages often add plausible hiring words around a brand name: career, talent, jobs, hiring, portal, or connect. A polished page on a new domain is still a new domain.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eTreat the sign-in step as the real checkpoint. If a page offers Google or Facebook login, check whether the flow actually opens a real provider-controlled authentication page. A fake window embedded inside the original site, broken controls, inactive links, or a provider logo used only as decoration should stop the process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUse security software that includes web and phishing protection. These tools can block known malicious pages before they load, which is especially useful when scam domains look polished, appear in search results, or are shared through otherwise legitimate channels.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eWhat companies can do\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eRecruitment pages are public by design, which makes them easy to copy.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity teams should monitor for newly registered domains combining their brand with hiring-related terms. They should also watch for pages that reuse favicons, recruiter photos, job descriptions, and calendar-booking language from legitimate hiring flows.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHR and recruiting teams should make the official hiring path easy to verify. Candidates should not have to guess whether a domain is legitimate. A clear careers page, consistent recruiter communication, and warnings about unofficial scheduling links can remove some of the ambiguity attackers rely on.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eConclusion\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fake FIFA hiring page worked because it did not feel unusual. It looked like a normal step in a hiring process.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAttackers do not need to invent new behavior when they can copy one people already trust. A branded scheduling page, a recruiter profile, and a familiar sign-in button can be enough to move a victim from interest to credential entry.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe decision point is the login. Before entering a work email or password, check the domain, check where the authentication flow really goes, and verify the role through the company’s official careers site. Security software with web and phishing protection adds another layer, especially against short-lived scam pages that may only stay online for hours or January 2026, we have detected 7.4 million malicious incidents tied to residential proxy traffic, affecting 572,000 users in our telemetry. In this model, the household whose connection is being used is often not the buyer of the traffic. It is the exit point.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat is the uncomfortable part of residential proxy networks. A home router, laptop, phone, smart TV, or other always-on device can quietly become part of a commercial network that routes strangers’ internet traffic through ordinary consumer IP addresses. To the websites on the other end, that traffic looks as if it came from a real household, because it did.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe technology itself is not inherently malicious. Businesses use residential proxies for market research, ad verification, brand protection, security testing, and services that need to see the web as a real user would. But the same feature that makes these networks useful also makes them attractive for abuse: traffic from a household IP address is hard to block without risking false positives against legitimate users.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe problem for consumers is simpler. Many people do not realize their device or connection can be enrolled into this kind of network at all. Some users opt in through pay-for-bandwidth apps. Others are enrolled through “free” VPNs, bundled software development kits inside unrelated apps, or unwanted software that installs proxy components without meaningful consent.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eHere is how the market works, who pays for it, and what it can cost the household that unknowingly hosts it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat is a residential proxy\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eA residential proxy is an internet connection – typically a home laptop, phone, smart TV, or router – that has been enrolled into a third-party network and is being used to route someone else's traffic. To the rest of the internet, that traffic looks like it is coming from an ordinary household IP address assigned by a consumer ISP, not from a datacenter or\u0026nbsp;VPN provider.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eResidential proxy networks are sold commercially. The model is two-sided: some people pay to tunnel their traffic out through someone else's connection (much like a VPN, except the exit point is a real home), while others get paid to provide those exit endpoints. Some are structured as paid peer-to-peer networks, where any participant's connection can serve as an exit for any other.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBuyers pay for access to pools of millions of these \"real user\" IPs because they are extremely difficult to block, or rather, blocking them carries real consequences: the traffic originates from genuine households, so any block risks shutting out a legitimate user – effectively a false positive.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eCommon buyers include market research firms, price-comparison services, brand-protection vendors, security teams testing their own defenses, and consumer privacy tools. At the abusive end of the market, the same infrastructure used by operators running automated purchasing bots, scrapping campaigns, ad fraud, credential stuffing, phishing and other attacks where hiding the true origin of the traffic matters. Demand is also being shaped by large-scale web scraping, including\u0026nbsp;\u003ca href=\"https://blog.includesecurity.com/2026/06/the-smart-tv-in-your-livingroom-is-a-node-in-the-aiscraping-economy/\"\u003escraping for AI training data\u003c/a\u003e. As anti-bot systems become better at identifying traffic from datacenters and known VPN providers, residential IP addresses become more attractive because they look like ordinary users. That does not make every residential proxy user malicious. It does mean consumers may be carrying traffic for businesses and actors they have never heard of.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eHow consumer devices end up in these networks\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are several ways a home device can become part of a residential proxy network. Some are explicit. Others are easy to miss.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe most obvious route is the pay-for-bandwidth model. Apps such as Honeygain, IPRoyal Pawns, and PacketStream pay users to share their internet connection. The user opts in, but they may not understand who will buy access to that connection later, what kind of traffic will pass through it, or what happens if that traffic triggers abuse reports.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA second route is the “free” VPN or proxy app. Instead of charging a subscription, the app monetizes the user’s connection by reselling bandwidth. In practice, the user may think they are getting a privacy tool while also becoming infrastructure for other people’s traffic.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eA third route is through bundled software development kits, or SDKs. A developer adds a third-party component to an otherwise unrelated app, such as a flashlight, wallpaper, game, or utility. The developer gets paid when the app is active. The user sees the host app, not the proxy network behind it.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fourth route is malware or potentially unwanted software. In those cases, the proxy component is installed silently or deceptively, with no meaningful user consent at all.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhy the \"consent\" is rarely meaningful\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eEven when consent technically exists, it is often not the kind of consent most people would recognize.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe disclosure may be buried in a long terms-of-service document. The wording may be vague, with phrases such as “share your unused bandwidth,” “support the app,” or “join our network.” These phrases do not clearly tell the user that arbitrary third parties may route traffic through the user’s home IP address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is also the question of downstream use. Users are rarely told who buys access to the network or what those buyers do with it. Their connection could be used for benign market research. It could also be used for scraping, automated account creation, ad fraud, credential stuffing, or phishing infrastructure.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe problem gets worse when the proxy component sits inside another app. The host app’s privacy policy may mention a partner SDK by brand name, but that still does not make it clear to a normal user that their device can become a proxy endpoint.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eUninstalling the visible app may not always resolve the issue cleanly either, especially when proxy components are installed separately or bundled with other unwanted software.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe risks consumers actually carry\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhen a household device becomes a residential proxy node, the user takes on risks created by someone else’s traffic.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe first risk is attribution. Abusive, fraudulent, or illegal activity routed through the device appears to come from the household’s IP address. ISP abuse notices, platform bans, fraud checks, and law enforcement requests point first to the subscriber, not to the person who bought the proxy traffic.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe second risk is performance. Continuous third-party traffic can consume bandwidth, slow the home network, hit data caps, or violate ISP terms of service. The user may only notice that the internet feels slower, a bill is higher, or a service suddenly blocks access.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe third risk is security exposure. Proxy clients run persistent background processes and maintain network connections so they can relay traffic. That adds always-on networking software to the device, often with limited visibility for the user.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fourth risk is reputation. A home IP address used for abuse can end up on blocklists used by banks, retailers, streaming services, and online platforms. The result can be account friction, failed logins, extra verification steps, or outright blocking from services the household actually uses.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is also a family angle. A child or another household member may install a free game, VPN, or utility without understanding the consequences. The legal, financial, and reputational risk still lands on the account holder.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat our telemetry shows\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eSince the beginning of 2026, we have detected 7.4 million malicious traffic incidents associated with residential proxy traffic, affecting 572,000 unique users in our user base.\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eThis is not a measurement of the entire residential proxy market. It reflects what we observe in our telemetry: malicious traffic, detections, and related activity affecting protected users. The real footprint is far larger, because residential proxy networks span major consumer platforms and many devices outside our visibility.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe activity is global, but it is concentrated in a smaller set of countries. India leads by a wide margin, with roughly 83,000 affected users, followed by Vietnam with 61,000 and Brazil with 39,000. The Philippines (23,000), Poland (18,000), Indonesia (18,000) and Thailand (14,000) form a second tier, with Egypt, Spain, Ukraine, Argentina, Mexico and Italy each accounting for 10,000 to 13,000. The spread across South and Southeast Asia, Latin America, and Eastern and Southern Europe reflects where the host apps that carry this software are most widely installed.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThat geographic spread likely reflects a combination of app distribution, local install patterns, user appetite for free or pay-for-bandwidth apps, and the economics of bandwidth resale. It should not be read as a simple ranking of where residential proxy abuse originates.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe chart below breaks down the threats we observed in this proxy-related traffic. Two categories dominate: phishing and malicious advertising. Behind them sits a long tail of consumer-facing fraud, including e-shop scams, generic scams, financial scams, and fake-tutorial pages, alongside outright malware such as trojans and other malicious the malicious proxy traffic we observed, scams and malware dominate. The important point for consumers is not only that their connection may be used by someone else. It is that, when abuse happens, their household IP address can be the return address.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eWhat consumers can do\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eMost people will not identify a residential proxy component by name. But there are practical warning signs.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eReview “free” VPNs, proxy apps, bandwidth-sharing apps, and utilities from unknown publishers. Check whether they mention bandwidth sharing, peer-to-peer networking, traffic routing, or network participation in their terms. If an app offers payment for leaving your device online, assume your connection may be resold unless the provider explains exactly how traffic is vetted and who can buy access.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn phones and computers, remove apps you do not recognize or no longer use. On home routers, check the list of connected devices and look for anything unfamiliar. If your ISP sends abuse notices, websites start blocking you, or your home IP address suddenly fails fraud checks, a proxy component is one possible explanation.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSecurity software can also help by detecting unwanted proxy clients, bundled components, and related malicious activity. But this should not be a guessing game for consumers. Software that turns a household connection into a commercial exit node should be named clearly and made easy to remove.\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eThe takeaway\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWith residential proxiesthe user’s connection becomes an infrastructure for someone else’s business.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe issue is not that residential proxy technology exists. It has legitimate uses. The issue is whether people know when their devices are part of it, understand what kind of traffic may pass through their connection, and have a real choice to opt out.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe fix is not to pretend consumers will read every EULA. This software should be named clearly, shown plainly, and made easy to remove before a household connection becomes someone else’s Points\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e43a143fd054561c50dba51071686adff\"\u003eGen Threat Labs has been tracking GoFlateLoader, a widespread Golang loader used to deliver multiple infostealers, including Amatera, Remus, Lumma, Vidar and StealC.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e3d3b5d0814ea5b26aa75a8d3d6013a39\"\u003eGoFlateLoader appears both in x86 (32-bit) and x86-64 (64-bit) variants, matching the bitness of the payload it is supposed to execute.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e51ccafcfb8c418d894aeb685004a0ba5\"\u003eThe loader is designed for in-memory payload execution and is deliberately inflated with a massive PE overlay to hinder detection\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ec4bafef8a1113d24bc2b4b3a943228d1\"\u003eThe two main delivery paths are via supposedly cracked software and a \u003ca href=\"https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/impersonation-click-hijacking-and-tds-inside-a-malware-distribution-ecosystem/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003emalicious TDS recently documented by Check Point Research\u003c/a\u003e, which may redirect users to dedicated landing pages serving password-protected archives containing GoFlateLoader.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch4\u003eIntroduction\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eNot every threat that matters is technically sophisticated, and that is also the case with GoFlateLoader, which is a rather simple loader written in Go, whose sole purpose is to decode and execute the payload in memory. What stands out the most is not what the loader does but rather what it does not do – it comes without anti-debugging, anti-VM, or sandbox-evasion checks, and also lacks API hashing or CFG obfuscation, the kind of tricks that loaders almost always come with. Instead, GoFlateLoader relies on one of the simplest yet still effective tricks to stay under the radar – it appends a massive PE overlay at the end of the file, deliberately inflating the binary's size (hence the name GoFlateLoader).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNevertheless, it is important to understand that malware does not necessarily need to be elegant or stealthy. In the end, the only thing that matters to attackers is whether it can successfully deliver the final payload. And if that is the only metric, GoFlateLoader appears to be working fairly well for its operators, as we continue to see it widely distributed across our user base. Since the beginning of April, we have protected more than 33,000 unique users from it, with the most affected countries including Brazil, India, Argentina, Mexico, Turkey and Spain.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2026-06/goflateloader_figure_1.jpg.webp?itok=vxCJJBzL 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2026-06/goflateloader_figure_1.jpg.webp?itok=1zvK3kgw 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":824},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2026-06/goflateloader_figure_1.jpg.webp?itok=NdkMl5Ch 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2026-06/goflateloader_figure_1.jpg.webp?itok=W-UCn4TK 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":627},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2026-06/goflateloader_figure_1.jpg.webp?itok=vVLcK_xU 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2026-06/goflateloader_figure_1.jpg.webp?itok=mYd8nteE 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":486}],"caption":"Figure 1: Global distribution of unique users protected by Gen from GoFlateLoader by country since April 2026. linear:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul type=\"disc\"\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"efa51af3b2859fd9c684efca0c82d3b66\"\u003eCopy the encoded payload blob from the \u003ccode\u003e.rdata\u003c/code\u003e section onto the stack.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e879bb970c6d1a81b8a42512222aceaa4\"\u003eDecode the payload using a small, multi-stage, custom byte-level transformation into a valid PE.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ebff4f340af15b75cbea0d682b26b7645\"\u003eParse the PE headers of the decoded payload (image base, image size, sections, data directories, etc.).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e37fb26cad3d18a4a0c8bf9b2595d661f\"\u003eAllocate an RWX memory region using \u003ccode\u003eVirtualAlloc\u003c/code\u003e with \u003ccode\u003eMEM_COMMIT | MEM_RESERVE\u003c/code\u003e and \u003ccode\u003ePAGE_EXECUTE_READWRITE\u003c/code\u003e flags, trying the payload’s preferred \u003ccode\u003eImageBase\u003c/code\u003e first and falling back to an OS-assigned address if that fails.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ebbde6d77cdc942c0a6bac79f4f8a6c28\"\u003eMap the decoded payload into the allocated memory region by copying the PE headers first, then walking the section table and writing each section to its target virtual address.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"ed16738229e0f5c70a981c0c5f9b715f9\"\u003eApply relocations (only if preferred base mapping fails).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli data-list-item-id=\"e76cde4bae1be6c21b897c350c6fd15db\"\u003eResolve imports by walking the standard \u003ccode\u003eIMAGE_IMPORT_DESCRIPTOR\u003c/code\u003e table and rebuild the IAT in place via \u003ccode\u003eLoadLibrary\u003c/code\u003e and\u003ccode\u003e GetProcAddress\u003c/code\u003e.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli In Go, \u003ccode\u003esyscall.Syscall\u003c/code\u003e has the signature \u003ccode\u003eSyscall(trap, nargs, a1, a2, a3)\u003c/code\u003e, where \u003ccode\u003etrap\u003c/code\u003e is the function pointer to invoke, \u003ccode\u003enargs \u003c/code\u003eis the argument count, and \u003ccode\u003ea1\u003c/code\u003e, \u003ccode\u003ea2\u003c/code\u003e, and \u003ccode\u003ea3\u003c/code\u003e are the first three arguments passed to the target function. Here, trap is set to point to the final payload’s entry point, \u003ccode\u003enargs\u003c/code\u003e is set to \u003ccode\u003e1\u003c/code\u003e, and since the execution is transferred to that entry point, the remaining arguments serve only as filler values and are never used by the invoked payload. is a deliberate design choice intended to bypass detection, as excessively large executable files may pose challenges for AVs and EDRs, primarily due to performance and resource constraints. As a result, to preserve performance and avoid noticeable impact to end users, these solutions often enforce practical file-size limits for deep scanning and/or emulation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe same logic extends to automated analysis pipelines – bloated files frequently cause timeouts during heuristic analysis and effectively prevent the sample from being uploaded to cloud-based sandboxes or threat intelligence platforms, which enforce their own strict upload limits to manage bandwidth and storage costs. A prime example is VirusTotal, which enforces a strict 650 MB upload limit. The fact that GoFlateLoader consistently sits just above this threshold strongly suggests that VirusTotal is one of the platforms it specifically aims to evade.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn most cases, GoFlateLoader’s PE overlays are filled with null bytes, though we have also observed builds with random adding such a PE overlay comes at virtually no distribution cost for attackers, as the inflated data compresses to a fraction of its original size (assuming constant padding).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eDelivered Final Payloads\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe final payloads distributed through GoFlateLoader are predominantly information stealers, with the most prevalent currently being Amatera, \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/research/remus-64bit-variant-of-lumma-stealer\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRemus\u003c/a\u003e, and Lumma. Beyond these, we have also observed it distributing Vidar, StealC, and SvitStealer.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGoFlateLoader comes in both x86 and x86-64 variants, with each variant matched to the architecture of the payload it is intended to execute.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAs for how GoFlateLoader binaries are distributed, we have observed at least two notable delivery paths. The first is through supposedly cracked software. The second involves the payload being served via \u003ca href=\"https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/impersonation-click-hijacking-and-tds-inside-a-malware-distribution-ecosystem/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ethe malicious TDS recently described by Check Point Research\u003c/a\u003e, which redirects users to a landing page that offers a downloadable archive and separately displays the password required to extract it. This approach provides several advantages for the threat actor. First, compression significantly reduces the binary size due to the constant-padded PE overlay. Second, because the archive password is displayed separately on the landing page, it makes it harder for AV and EDR solutions to obtain it and decrypt the archive. As a result, the payload may remain concealed until the victim manually extracts it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eSummary\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eGoFlateLoader is a reminder that prevalence and sophistication are not the same thing. At its core, it is a rather simple manual in-memory PE loader written in Go, with no novel anti-analysis tricks under the hood. Whatever evasion it achieves comes not from obfuscation or other anti-analysis tricks but from its size – the appended PE overlay is built to slip past size-constrained scanners and automated analysis pipelines. Yet, despite its low level of sophistication, GoFlateLoader remains an active and relevant threat, used to deliver a wide range of prevalent information stealers.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGen products protect our users against both GoFlateLoader and the payloads it delivers.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch4\u003eIndicators of Compromise (IoCs)\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h4\u003e\u003cp\u003eNote: The files listed in the IoCs that are
